Being intersecting, $\mathcal F$ contains at most one of $I_i$, $I_{i-k}$. So at most $1+(k-1)=k$ arcs of the arrangement are in $\mathcal F$.

**Step 2: double counting.** Over all $n!$ arrangements, the number $N$ of pairs (arrangement, arc in $\mathcal F$) satisfies $N\le k\cdot n!$. On the other hand, a given set $A\in\mathcal F$ is an arc in exactly $n\,k!\,(n-k)!$ arrangements. So
$$N=|\mathcal F|\cdot n\,k!\,(n-k)!\le k\cdot n!\ \Longrightarrow\ |\mathcal F|\le\frac kn\binom nk=\binom{n-1}{k-1}.$$
∎

**Sharpness.** All $k$-sets containing the element $1$ form an intersecting family of size $\binom{n-1}{k-1}$.

**Lesson.** A cyclic arrangement turns a global set-system problem into a local one; then double count.
@@@ comb2-a-seven-by-seven-matrix-without-a-rectangle.md
---
title: A 7 × 7 grid with no rectangle of marked cells
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: double counting, convexity, Fano plane, extremal
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Some cells of a $7\times7$ board are marked so that no four marked cells are the corners of a rectangle with sides parallel to the grid. What is the largest possible number of marked cells?

## Hint

Count pairs of marked cells that lie in the same row. Each pair of columns can occur in at most one row. Then use convexity.

## Solution

**Idea.** Count (row, pair of marked columns in that row).

**Upper bound.** Let $r_i$ be the number of marked cells in row $i$, and $T=\sum r_i$. A pair of columns can appear in at most one row. There are $\binom72=21$ pairs of columns, so
$$\sum_{i=1}^7\binom{r_i}2\le21.$$
By convexity, $\sum\binom{r_i}2\ge\frac{T(T-7)}{14}$. If $T\ge22$, this is at least $\frac{22\cdot15}{14}>23>21$, a contradiction. So $T\le21$.

**Construction with $21$.** Number rows and columns $0,\dots,6$ and mark in row $i$ the columns $i,\ i+1,\ i+3\pmod7$. The set $\{0,1,3\}$ is a perfect difference set modulo $7$: any two rows share exactly one marked column, so there is no rectangle; $7\cdot3=21$ marked cells.

**Answer.** $21$. (This is the incidence matrix of the Fano plane.)

**Lesson.** Count pairs inside each row, apply convexity, and look for a design that achieves the bound.
@@@ comb2-the-fifteen-puzzle-parity.md
---
title: The 15-puzzle with two tiles swapped
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: invariant, parity, inversions, puzzle
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

The 15-puzzle has $15$ numbered tiles in a $4\times4$ frame with one empty cell; a move slides a tile into the empty cell. Starting from the solved position, can we reach the position in which tiles $14$ and $15$ are swapped and everything else is as before?

## Hint

Read the tiles row by row, ignoring the empty cell, and count inversions. Find a quantity combining the number of inversions with the row of the empty cell that is preserved by every move.

## Solution

**Idea.** Find an invariant that mixes the parity of inversions with the row of the blank.

**Invariant.** For a position, let $N$ be the number of inversions of the sequence of the $15$ tiles read row by row, and let $r\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ be the row of the empty cell from the top. The parity of $N+r$ does not change under a move.
- **Horizontal move.** $N$ and $r$ are unchanged.
- **Vertical move.** $r$ changes by $\pm1$. The moved tile jumps over exactly $3$ other tiles, so $N$ changes by an odd number. Hence $N+r$ keeps its parity.

**Conclusion.** The solved position has $N=0$ and $r=4$, so $N+r$ is even. The position with tiles $14,15$ swapped has $N=1$ and $r=4$, so $N+r$ is odd. Since moves preserve the parity, this position cannot be reached. ∎

**Remark.** Sam Loyd's famous unsolvable puzzle. Exactly half of all positions are reachable.

**Lesson.** Parity of inversions is the invariant of sliding puzzles; correct it with the position of the blank.
@@@ comb2-nim-and-bouton-s-theorem.md
---
title: Nim and the XOR rule
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 3
tags: combinatorial games, Nim, XOR, binary
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

In the game of Nim there are several piles of stones. Two players alternately remove any positive number of stones from one pile; the player who cannot move loses. Prove that the player to move wins if and only if the bitwise XOR of the pile sizes is non-zero. Which move wins from $(3,4,5)$?

## Hint

Show that from XOR $=0$ every move produces XOR $\ne0$, and that from XOR $\ne0$ there is a move to XOR $=0$ (look at the leading bit of the XOR).

## Solution

**Idea.** Call a position *balanced* if the XOR $s$ of the pile sizes is $0$. Show that balanced positions are exactly the losing ones.

**Balanced to unbalanced.** If the position is balanced and a pile $a$ is replaced by $a'<a$, the new XOR is $a\oplus a'\ne0$.

**Unbalanced to balanced.** Let $s\ne0$ and let $t$ be the position of its leading bit. Some pile $a$ has bit $t$ set. Replace $a$ by $a'=a\oplus s$. Since $s$ has leading bit $t$ and $a$ has that bit equal to $1$, flipping it makes $a'<a$, so removing $a-a'$ stones is legal, and the new XOR is $s\oplus a\oplus a'=0$.

**Conclusion.** The terminal position is balanced. A player facing a balanced position can only move to unbalanced positions; so the player facing a balanced position loses. ∎

**Example.** $3\oplus4\oplus5=2\ne0$. The pile $3$ has the bit of value $2$: replace it by $3\oplus2=1$, i.e. remove $2$ stones. The position $(1,4,5)$ has XOR $0$ ✓.

**Lesson.** For impartial games, find the invariant set of losing positions and prove the two closure properties.
@@@ comb2-a-subtraction-game-with-1-3-4.md
---
title: A subtraction game with moves 1, 3 and 4
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: combinatorial games, losing positions, induction, periodicity
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

There are $n$ stones. Two players alternately remove $1$, $3$ or $4$ stones; the player who cannot move loses. Determine for which $n$ the first player has a winning strategy. Who wins with $100$ stones?

## Hint

Compute the first few losing positions: $0$ is losing. Guess a period and prove it by induction.

## Solution

**Idea.** A position is losing iff all its moves lead to winning positions. Compute the losing positions and find the pattern.

**Claim.** The losing positions are exactly the $n$ with $n\equiv0$ or $2\pmod7$.

**Proof by strong induction on $n$.**
- *From a position in $L$ every move leads outside $L$:* if $n\equiv0$, the moves lead to $n-1\equiv6$, $n-3\equiv4$, $n-4\equiv3$; if $n\equiv2$, they lead to $n-1\equiv1$, $n-3\equiv6$, $n-4\equiv5$. None of these is $0$ or $2$ mod $7$.
- *From a position outside $L$ there is a move into $L$:* $n\equiv1$: remove $1$; $n\equiv3$: remove $1$; $n\equiv4$: remove $4$; $n\equiv5$: remove $3$; $n\equiv6$: remove $4$.

Together with $n=0$ losing, positions in $L$ are exactly the losing ones.

**Answer.** The first player wins iff $n\not\equiv0,2\pmod7$. For $n=100\equiv2\pmod7$ the first player **loses**.

**Lesson.** Compute small cases, guess the periodic set of losing positions, then verify the two properties.
@@@ comb2-chomp-and-strategy-stealing.md
---
title: Chomp and the strategy-stealing argument
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: combinatorial games, strategy stealing, Chomp, invariant
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

A rectangular chocolate bar has $m$ rows and $n$ columns of squares. The bottom-left square is poisoned. Two players alternately choose a remaining square and eat it together with all squares above and to its right. The player who eats the poisoned square loses.

1. Prove that if the bar has more than one square, the first player has a winning strategy.
2. For a bar with $2$ rows, describe the winning strategy explicitly.

## Hint

1. Consider the move that eats only the top-right square. 2. After the first move, try to keep the bottom row exactly one square longer than the top row.

## Solution

**Idea.** The game is finite and has no draws, so exactly one of the players has a winning strategy.

**1. Strategy stealing.** The first player considers the move $M$ that eats only the top-right corner square.
- If $M$ leads to a position that is losing for the opponent, the first player wins by playing $M$.
- Otherwise, the opponent has a winning reply $X$. The square chosen by $X$ was present at the start, and eating it together with everything above and to its right also removes the top-right corner square, so playing $X$ at the very start leads to the same position as $M$ followed by $X$. That position is losing for the player to move, so the first player wins by playing $X$ at once.

In both cases the first player has a winning strategy.

**2. Two rows, explicit strategy.** Let the top row have $t$ squares and the bottom row $b\ge t$. The first player starts by eating the top-right square, creating $t=n-1$, $b=n$. He keeps the invariant $b=t+1$ after each of his moves, until he reaches $t=0$, $b=1$, after which the opponent has to eat the poisoned square. Suppose the opponent faces $b=t+1$ and moves:
- he eats in the top row from column $c$: now $t'=c-1$ and $b=t+1\ge c+1$. The first player eats the bottom row from column $c+1$: the bottom row becomes $c$, restoring $b=t'+1$;
- he eats in the bottom row from column $c$ ($2\le c\le b$): the bottom row has $c-1$ squares and the top row has $c-1$ squares. The first player eats the end of the top row (column $c-1$, if $c\ge2$), leaving $t'=c-2$ and $b'=c-1$, so $b'=t'+1$ again.
The total number of squares decreases at each turn, so eventually the opponent is left with the poisoned square.

**Remark.** For rectangles with at least three rows and columns no explicit winning strategy is known in general.

**Lesson.** Strategy stealing proves existence; explicit strategies come from maintaining an invariant shape.
@@@ comb2-five-points-contain-a-convex-quadrilateral.md
---
title: Five points contain a convex quadrilateral
category: combi/pigeonhole
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: combinatorial geometry, convex position, pigeonhole, Happy Ending
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Five points are given in the plane, no three on a line. Prove that four of them are the vertices of a convex quadrilateral.

## Hint

Consider the convex hull of the five points. How many vertices can it have? If it is a triangle, look at the line through the two interior points.

## Solution

**Idea.** Split according to the number of vertices of the convex hull.

**Hull with $4$ or $5$ vertices.** Then any $4$ hull vertices are in convex position, and we are done.

**Hull a triangle $ABC$.** The other two points $D,E$ are inside the triangle. The line $DE$ does not pass through $A,B,C$, so each of $A,B,C$ is on one side of it. By pigeonhole, two of them, say $B$ and $C$, lie on the same side of $DE$.

We claim $B,C,E,D$ are in convex position.
- $B$ and $C$ are vertices of the hull, so they cannot lie in the convex hull of other points.
- $D$ is not in triangle $BCE$: this triangle touches the line $DE$ only at $E$, while $D\ne E$ is on that line. Symmetrically $E$ is not in triangle $BCD$.

So the quadrilateral with vertices $B,C,E,D$ is convex. ∎

**Remark.** This is the first case of the Erdős–Szekeres "Happy Ending" problem.

**Lesson.** Case on the convex hull, then use pigeonhole on which side of a well-chosen line the vertices lie.
@@@ comb2-sylvester-gallai-by-kelly.md
---
title: The Sylvester–Gallai theorem
category: combi/pigeonhole
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: combinatorial geometry, extremal principle, minimal distance
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Let $S$ be a finite set of points in the plane, not all on one line. Prove that there is a line containing exactly two points of $S$.

## Hint

Among all pairs (point $P$ of $S$, line $\ell$ through at least two points of $S$ with $P\notin\ell$), choose one with the smallest distance from $P$ to $\ell$. Show that $\ell$ contains no third point.

## Solution

**Idea (Kelly).** Minimise a distance, then find a smaller one if the line has three points.

**Setup.** Since the points are not all collinear, such pairs $(P,\ell)$ exist, and there are finitely many, so one pair minimises $d(P,\ell)=PQ>0$.

**Claim.** $\ell$ contains exactly two points of $S$. Suppose it contains at least three. Among three points of $\ell$, two (say $B\ne C$) lie on the same side of $Q$ on $\ell$; label them so that $B$ lies between $Q$ and $C$. Then $BC\le QC<PC$, because $PC$ is the hypotenuse of the right triangle $PQC$.

Consider the line $m=PC$ (it contains the two points $P,C$ of $S$) and the point $B\in S$. $B$ is not on $m$. The distance from $B$ to $m$ is
$$d(B,m)=BC\sin\angle PCQ=BC\cdot\frac{PQ}{PC}<PQ,$$
contradicting the minimality of $PQ$. Hence $\ell$ contains exactly two points of $S$. ∎

**Remark.** Finiteness is needed: for the set of all points with rational coordinates every line through two points contains infinitely many.

**Lesson.** Extremal principle: pick the configuration minimising a distance, then show a violation would produce a smaller one.
@@@ comb2-conway-s-soldiers.md
---
title: Conway's soldiers cannot reach the fifth row
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: weight function, potential, golden ratio, infinite board
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

Soldiers occupy every lattice point $(x,y)$ with $y\le0$ of the plane, one per point. A move: a soldier jumps over an adjacent soldier (horizontally or vertically) onto the empty point just behind, and the soldier jumped over is removed. Prove that no finite sequence of moves can bring a soldier to the point $(0,5)$.

## Hint

Give each lattice point $(x,y)$ the weight $\sigma^{|x|+(5-y)}$ with $\sigma=\frac{\sqrt5-1}2$ (so $\sigma^2+\sigma=1$). Show that the total weight of the soldiers never increases and that the initial total weight is exactly $1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Use a potential function with the golden ratio so that jumps toward the target keep the total unchanged and all other jumps decrease it.

**Weights.** Let $\sigma=\frac{\sqrt5-1}2\approx0.618$, so $\sigma^2=1-\sigma$. The weight of the point $P=(x,y)$ is $\sigma^{d(P)}$ with $d(P)=|x|+|5-y|$. The target $T=(0,5)$ has weight $1$.

**Initial weight.** Using $\sum_{k\ge0}\sigma^k=\frac1{1-\sigma}=\frac1{\sigma^2}$ and $\sum_{x\in\mathbb Z}\sigma^{|x|}=1+\frac{2\sigma}{1-\sigma}=1+\frac2\sigma$, for $y\le0$,
$$W_0=\sigma^5\cdot\frac1{\sigma^2}\left(1+\frac2\sigma\right)=\sigma^3+2\sigma^2=1.$$

**A move does not increase the weight.** A move takes three consecutive points $P,Q,R$ on a line and replaces soldiers on $P,Q$ by one on $R$. Along a line, the taxicab distance to $T$ is convex, so the triple $(d(P),d(Q),d(R))$ has one of the forms:
- $(n+2,n+1,n)$: change $\sigma^n-\sigma^{n+1}-\sigma^{n+2}=\sigma^n(1-\sigma-\sigma^2)=0$;
- $(n,n+1,n+2)$: change $<0$;
- $(n+1,n,n+1)$: change $<0$.
So the total weight never increases.

**Conclusion.** After any finite sequence of moves the weight is at most $W_0=1$. Suppose a soldier reached $T$. It contributes weight $1$; but infinitely many other soldiers remain, each with positive weight, so the total would exceed $1$. Contradiction. ∎

**Remark.** The golden ratio is exactly the value making jumps toward the target neutral, which is why row $4$ can be reached but row $5$ cannot.

**Lesson.** Weight/potential functions: choose weights that make the dangerous move neutral and every other move a decrease.
@@@ comb2-hall-s-marriage-theorem-and-a-deck-of-cards.md
---
title: Hall's marriage theorem and a deck of cards
category: combi/graphs
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: Hall's theorem, matchings, bipartite graphs, induction
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

1. (Hall) Let $X$ and $Y$ be the two sides of a bipartite graph. Suppose that for every subset $A\subseteq X$, $|N(A)|\ge|A|$. Prove that there is a matching that covers all of $X$.
2. The $52$ cards of a deck (13 ranks, four cards of each rank) are dealt into $13$ piles of $4$ cards. Prove that one can choose one card from each pile so that the $13$ chosen cards have $13$ different ranks.

## Hint

For 1, induct on $|X|$. Distinguish whether every proper non-empty $A$ has $|N(A)|>|A|$ or whether some $A$ has $|N(A)|=|A|$. For 2, let $X$ be the set of piles and $Y$ the set of ranks.

## Solution

**1. Induction on $|X|$.** If $|X|=1$, the single vertex has a neighbour.

Let $|X|\ge2$.

*Case 1: $|N(A)|\ge|A|+1$ for every non-empty proper subset $A\subset X$.* Pick $x\in X$ and $y\in N(x)$; match them and delete both. For any non-empty $A\subseteq X\setminus\{x\}$, $|N'(A)|\ge|N(A)|-1\ge|A|$, so Hall's condition still holds and induction gives a matching of the rest.

*Case 2: some non-empty proper $A\subset X$ has $|N(A)|=|A|$.* By induction $A$ can be matched into $N(A)$. In the remaining graph, for $B\subseteq X\setminus A$,
$$|N(B)\setminus N(A)|=|N(A\cup B)|-|N(A)|\ge|B|,$$
so Hall's condition holds and induction matches $X\setminus A$. The two matchings together cover $X$. ∎

**2. The cards.** Let $X$ be the piles, $Y$ the ranks, and join a pile to a rank if the pile contains a card of that rank. Take any $k$ piles: they contain $4k$ cards, and each rank has exactly $4$ cards, so these piles contain cards of at least $k$ distinct ranks. By Hall there is a matching assigning to each pile a distinct rank present in it. ∎

**Lesson.** When you need a system of distinct representatives, check Hall's condition.
@@@ comb2-sperner-s-theorem-by-lym.md
---
title: Sperner's theorem and the LYM inequality
category: combi/binomial
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: antichains, double counting, chains, Sperner
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

A family $\mathcal F$ of subsets of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ is an *antichain* if no set of $\mathcal F$ is contained in another. Prove the LYM inequality
$$\sum_{A\in\mathcal F}\frac1{\binom n{|A|}}\le1,$$
and deduce Sperner's theorem: $|\mathcal F|\le\dbinom n{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$.

## Hint

Count pairs (set $A\in\mathcal F$, maximal chain $\emptyset=C_0\subset C_1\subset\dots\subset C_n=\{1,\dots,n\}$ passing through $A$).

## Solution

**Idea.** A maximal chain adds one element at a time, so it corresponds to a permutation; an antichain meets each chain at most once.

**LYM.** There are $n!$ maximal chains. Each set $A$ of size $a$ lies on exactly $a!\,(n-a)!$ of them. Since $\mathcal F$ is an antichain, each chain contains at most one member of $\mathcal F$. Therefore
$$\sum_{A\in\mathcal F}|A|!\,(n-|A|)!\le n!.$$
Dividing by $n!$ gives $\sum_{A\in\mathcal F}\dfrac1{\binom n{|A|}}\le1$.

**Sperner.** For every $a$, $\binom na\le\binom n{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}$, so
$$\frac{|\mathcal F|}{\binom n{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}\le\sum_{A\in\mathcal F}\frac1{\binom n{|A|}}\le1.$$
∎

**Sharpness.** All subsets of size $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$ form an antichain of that size.

**Lesson.** Double count (set, maximal chain) pairs: chains correspond to permutations and give clean factorial weights.
@@@ comb2-a-large-sum-free-subset.md
---
title: Every set of n integers contains a sum-free subset of more than n/3 elements
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 5
tags: probabilistic method, averaging, sum-free sets, modular arithmetic
date: 2026-11-05
---

## Statement

A set of integers is *sum-free* if it contains no $a,b,c$ (with $a=b$ allowed) such that $a+b=c$. Prove that every set $A$ of $n$ non-zero integers has a sum-free subset with more than $\frac n3$ elements.

## Hint

Pick a prime $p=3k+2$ larger than every $|a|$, $a\in A$. The residues $k+1,\dots,2k+1$ form a sum-free set modulo $p$. Choose a random $x$ and keep the elements $a$ with $ax\bmod p$ in that middle third; compute the expected size.

## Solution

**Idea.** Multiply by a random unit modulo a prime and keep the elements landing in a sum-free "middle third".

**A suitable prime.** There are infinitely many primes $p\equiv2\pmod3$. Choose such a prime $p=3k+2$ with $p>|a|$ for all $a\in A$; then no $a$ is divisible by $p$.

**The middle third.** Let $S=\{k+1,k+2,\dots,2k+1\}\subset\mathbb Z_p$, so $|S|=k+1$. If $u,v\in S$, then $2k+2\le u+v\le4k+2$. Modulo $p=3k+2$ these sums are the residues $2k+2,\dots,3k+1$ and $0,\dots,k$. None lies in $S$. So $S$ is sum-free modulo $p$.

**Averaging.** For $x$ uniform in $\{1,\dots,p-1\}$ and fixed $a\in A$, $ax\bmod p$ is uniform on the non-zero residues. So
$$\Pr[ax\in S]=\frac{k+1}{3k+1}>\frac13.$$
By linearity, the expected number of $a\in A$ with $ax\bmod p\in S$ is more than $\frac n3$. Hence some $x$ gives $B=\{a\in A:ax\bmod p\in S\}$ with more than $\frac n3$ elements.

**$B$ is sum-free.** If $a+b=c$ with $a,b,c\in B$, then multiplying by $x$ gives $ax+bx\equiv cx\pmod p$ with $ax\bmod p,\ bx\bmod p,\ cx\bmod p\in S$, contradicting the sum-freeness of $S$. ∎

**Lesson.** Randomise by a dilation in a finite field to bring a hard structure into a nice fixed set, and use linearity of expectation.
@@@ comb-two-people-with-the-same-number-of-friends.md
---
title: Two people with the same number of friends
category:  combi/pigeonhole
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: pigeonhole, graphs, degrees
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

At a party with $n\ge2$ people, friendship is mutual. Prove that two people have the same number of friends at the party.

## Hint

The number of friends of a person is an integer between $0$ and $n-1$. That gives $n$ possible values for $n$ people, which is one too many to conclude directly. Can the values $0$ and $n-1$ both occur?

## Solution

**Idea.** Count the possible values and show that there are really only $n-1$ of them.

**Values.** Each person has between $0$ and $n-1$ friends. Suppose some person $A$ has $0$ friends and some person $B$ has $n-1$ friends. Then $B$ is a friend of everybody, in particular of $A$, so $A$ has at least one friend: contradiction. Hence the values $0$ and $n-1$ do not occur together.

**Pigeonhole.**
- If nobody has $0$ friends, all the numbers of friends lie in $\{1,\dots,n-1\}$.
- If somebody has $0$ friends, then nobody has $n-1$ friends, and all the numbers lie in $\{0,\dots,n-2\}$.

In both cases the $n$ people take at most $n-1$ different values, so two people have the same number of friends.

**Remark.** The statement is false if we allow a person to be their own friend or friendships to be one-way. The mutuality is used exactly once, in the key step.

**Lesson.** Pigeonhole: count the boxes carefully, and look for a hidden constraint that removes one box.
@@@ comb-a-block-of-consecutive-terms-divisible-by-n.md
---
title: A block of consecutive terms divisible by n
category:  combi/pigeonhole
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: pigeonhole, prefix sums, remainders
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $a_1,a_2,\dots,a_n$ be integers. Prove that there exist $i\le j$ such that the sum $a_i+a_{i+1}+\dots+a_j$ is divisible by $n$.

## Hint

Consider the $n+1$ partial sums $S_0=0$, $S_1=a_1$, $S_2=a_1+a_2$, $\dots$, $S_n$ and their remainders modulo $n$.

## Solution

**Idea.** A block sum is a difference of two partial sums.

**Pigeonhole.** The $n+1$ numbers $S_0,S_1,\dots,S_n$ have only $n$ possible remainders modulo $n$, so two of them, say $S_p$ and $S_q$ with $p<q$, have the same remainder. Then $n$ divides
$$S_q-S_p=a_{p+1}+a_{p+2}+\dots+a_q.$$
Taking $i=p+1$ and $j=q$ gives the required block.

**Remark.** The result is sharp: for $n-1$ numbers it fails. Take $a_1=\dots=a_{n-1}=1$.

**Lesson.** Turn sums of blocks into differences of prefix sums, then apply the pigeonhole principle to remainders.
@@@ comb-monotone-subsequences-erdos-szekeres.md
---
title: Monotone subsequences (Erdős–Szekeres)
category:  combi/pigeonhole
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: pigeonhole, sequences, Erdős–Szekeres
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge1$. Prove that every sequence of $n^2+1$ distinct real numbers contains an increasing subsequence of length $n+1$ or a decreasing subsequence of length $n+1$.

## Hint

To each term $a_i$ attach the pair $(x_i,y_i)$, where $x_i$ is the length of the longest increasing subsequence ending at $a_i$ and $y_i$ the length of the longest decreasing subsequence ending at $a_i$. Show that these pairs are pairwise distinct.

## Solution

**Idea.** Label every term by two numbers, and show that the labels never repeat.

**Distinct labels.** Let $i<j$.
- If $a_i<a_j$, any increasing subsequence ending at $a_i$ can be extended by $a_j$, so $x_j\ge x_i+1$.
- If $a_i>a_j$, any decreasing subsequence ending at $a_i$ can be extended by $a_j$, so $y_j\ge y_i+1$.

In both cases $(x_i,y_i)\ne(x_j,y_j)$.

**Counting.** Suppose, for contradiction, that all increasing and decreasing subsequences have length at most $n$. Then $1\le x_i,y_i\le n$ for all $i$, so there are at most $n^2$ possible pairs. But we have $n^2+1$ terms with pairwise distinct pairs: contradiction.

**Remark.** The bound $n^2+1$ is sharp.

**Lesson.** When pigeonhole directly fails, build a cleverly chosen label for each object so that distinct objects get distinct labels.
@@@ comb-one-number-divides-another.md
---
title: Choosing n + 1 numbers among 1, …, 2n
category:  combi/pigeonhole
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: pigeonhole, divisibility, odd part
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Let $n\ge1$ and let $S$ be a set of $n+1$ distinct integers chosen from $\{1,2,\dots,2n\}$. Prove that $S$ contains two numbers such that one divides the other. Show that the statement fails for sets of $n$ numbers.

## Hint

Write every integer as $2^k\cdot m$ with $m$ odd. How many possible values of $m$ are there?

## Solution

**Idea.** Sort the numbers into boxes according to their odd part.

**Boxes.** Every $a\in\{1,\dots,2n\}$ can be written uniquely as $a=2^km$ with $k\ge0$ and $m$ odd. The odd part $m$ is one of the $n$ odd numbers $1,3,\dots,2n-1$.

**Pigeonhole.** The $n+1$ chosen numbers go into $n$ boxes, so two of them, $a=2^km$ and $b=2^lm$ with $a\ne b$, share the same odd part $m$. Then $k\ne l$, and if $k<l$ then $a$ divides $b$; otherwise $b$ divides $a$.

**Sharpness.** The $n$ numbers $n+1,n+2,\dots,2n$ contain no pair where one divides the other.

**Lesson.** Decompose objects into a canonical form and use one component as the pigeonhole box.
@@@ comb-numbers-on-a-board-parity-invariant.md
---
title: Replacing two numbers by their difference
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: invariant, parity, board games
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

The numbers $1,2,\dots,10$ are written on a board. A move consists of erasing two numbers $a$ and $b$ and writing $|a-b|$ instead. After nine moves, a single number remains. Prove that it is odd.

## Hint

How does the parity of the sum of all numbers on the board change after a move?

## Solution

**Idea.** Find a quantity that a move cannot change, at least modulo $2$.

**Invariant.** A move replaces the two numbers $a,b$ by $|a-b|$, so the sum $T$ of the numbers on the board changes by
$$-(a+b)+|a-b|=-2\min(a,b),$$
which is even. Hence the parity of $T$ never changes.

**Conclusion.** At the start $T=1+2+\dots+10=55$, which is odd. After nine moves, $T$ is the single remaining number, and it is odd.

**Remark.** The same argument works for $1,\dots,n$: the final number has the parity of $\frac{n(n+1)}2$.

**Lesson.** Invariant: find a quantity (often a parity) that every allowed move preserves.
@@@ comb-the-parliament-with-enemies.md
---
title: Splitting a parliament into two houses
category: combi/graphs
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: monovariant, graphs, extremal, process
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

In a parliament, each member has at most $3$ enemies, and enmity is mutual. Prove that the members can be split into two houses so that every member has at most $1$ enemy in his own house.

## Hint

Start with an arbitrary split. If somebody has at least $2$ enemies in his own house, move him to the other house. Consider the number of pairs of enemies who sit in the same house.

## Solution

**Idea.** Use a monovariant: a nonnegative integer that strictly decreases at each step.

**Process.** Start with any split of the members into two houses. Let $E$ be the number of pairs of enemies belonging to the same house. If every member has at most one enemy in his own house, we are done. Otherwise, take a member $x$ with at least $2$ enemies in his own house.

**Effect of the move.** Since $x$ has at most $3$ enemies in total, he has at most $1$ enemy in the other house. Move $x$ to the other house.
- The pairs $\{x,y\}$ with $y$ an enemy in his former house are no longer in the same house: at least $2$ pairs are removed from the count.
- The pairs $\{x,y\}$ with $y$ an enemy in the other house now count: at most $1$ new pair.
- All other pairs are unaffected.

Hence $E$ decreases by at least $1$.

**Termination.** $E$ is a nonnegative integer, so the process cannot continue forever. When it stops, nobody has $2$ or more enemies in his own house.

**Lesson.** Monovariant: to prove a configuration with some property exists, start anywhere and improve a nonnegative integer quantity until it cannot be improved.
@@@ comb-dominoes-on-a-mutilated-chessboard.md
---
title: Dominoes on a mutilated chessboard
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: colouring, tilings, dominoes
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Two opposite corner squares are removed from an $8\times8$ chessboard. Can the remaining $62$ squares be tiled by $31$ dominoes ($1\times2$ rectangles)?

## Hint

Use the usual black and white colouring and count the squares of each colour.

## Solution

**Idea.** Every domino covers exactly one black and one white square.

**Colouring.** Colour the board like a chessboard. Two opposite corners, for instance $(1,1)$ and $(8,8)$, have the same colour: $1+1$ and $8+8$ are both even.

**Counting.** The full board has $32$ black and $32$ white squares. Removing two squares of the same colour leaves $32$ of one colour and $30$ of the other.

**Contradiction.** Any tiling by $31$ dominoes covers exactly $31$ black and $31$ white squares. Impossible.

**Answer.** No.

**Remark.** If the two removed squares have different colours, a tiling always exists (Gomory's theorem).

**Lesson.** Colouring: choose a colouring in which every tile covers a prescribed number of squares of each colour, then compare with the colour counts of the region.
@@@ comb-tetrominoes-on-a-ten-by-ten-board.md
---
title: Tiling a 10 × 10 board with 1 × 4 tiles
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: colouring, tilings, tetrominoes, modular colouring
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Is it possible to tile a $10\times10$ board with $25$ rectangles of size $1\times4$ (each placed horizontally or vertically)?

## Hint

The chessboard colouring is useless here. Try four colours, according to the remainder of $i+j$ modulo $4$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Choose a colouring for which every tile covers one square of each colour.

**Colouring.** Give square $(i,j)$ the colour $(i+j)\bmod4$. A horizontal or vertical tile covers four consecutive residues modulo $4$: all different.

**Consequence.** A tiling by $25$ tiles would cover exactly $25$ squares of each colour.

**Counting.** Counting the squares of each colour on the $10\times10$ board gives colour $0$: $25$; colour $1$: $24$; colour $2$: $25$; colour $3$: $26$. Since colour $3$ has $26$ squares, a tiling is impossible.

**Answer.** No.

**Remark.** The argument adapts to every $n\times n$ board: a tiling by $1\times4$ tiles exists if and only if $4$ divides $n$.

**Lesson.** When a tile has length $k$, colour the board with $k$ colours along diagonals; each tile then covers all the colours once.
@@@ comb-no-finite-set-of-midpoints.md
---
title: A finite set in which every point is a midpoint
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: extremal principle, plane geometry, midpoints
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Prove that there is no finite non-empty set $S$ of points in the plane such that every point of $S$ is the midpoint of two other points of $S$.

## Hint

Look at the point of $S$ that is largest in some sense: for instance, largest abscissa, and among those the largest ordinate.

## Solution

**Idea.** An extreme point can never be the midpoint of two other points.

**Extreme point.** Choose the point $P=(p,q)$ of $S$ with the largest abscissa $p$, and, among the points with abscissa $p$, the largest ordinate $q$.

**Contradiction.** Suppose $P$ is the midpoint of two other points $A=(a_1,a_2)$ and $B=(b_1,b_2)$ of $S$. Then $\frac{a_1+b_1}2=p$ with $a_1\le p$ and $b_1\le p$, so $a_1=b_1=p$. Now $A$ and $B$ both have abscissa $p$, hence $a_2\le q$ and $b_2\le q$, and $\frac{a_2+b_2}2=q$ forces $a_2=b_2=q$. Then $A=B=P$, but $A$ and $B$ were supposed to differ from $P$. Contradiction.

**Remark.** For an infinite set the statement is false: $\mathbb{Z}^2$ has this property.

**Lesson.** Extremal principle: look at a point that maximises or minimises some quantity.
@@@ comb-tiling-a-board-with-l-trominoes.md
---
title: Tiling a 2ⁿ × 2ⁿ board with L-trominoes
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: induction, tilings, trominoes, divide and conquer
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

An L-tromino is the shape made of three unit squares forming an "L". Prove that for every $n\ge1$, a $2^n\times2^n$ board from which any single square has been removed can be tiled by L-trominoes.

## Hint

Divide the board into four quadrants. After placing one L-tromino well, each quadrant becomes an instance of the problem for $n-1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Induction on $n$, with a first move that recreates four smaller copies of the same problem.

**Base case.** For $n=1$, a $2\times2$ board minus one square is exactly one L-tromino.

**Induction step.** Assume the statement for $n-1$ and take a $2^n\times2^n$ board with one square removed. Divide it into four quadrants of size $2^{n-1}\times2^{n-1}$. The removed square lies in one of them.

Consider the four squares at the centre of the board: one in each quadrant. Place one L-tromino covering the three central squares that lie in the three quadrants **not** containing the removed square.

Now each quadrant has exactly one square that is missing. By the induction hypothesis, each quadrant minus that square can be tiled by L-trominoes. Together with the central tromino, this tiles the whole board.

**Remark.** The count is consistent: $4^n-1$ is divisible by $3$.

**Lesson.** Induction with a self-similar structure: choose a first step that reduces the problem to several smaller problems of exactly the same kind.
@@@ comb-regions-cut-by-lines-in-the-plane.md
---
title: Regions cut by n lines
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: induction, counting, lines in the plane
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

$n$ lines are drawn in the plane so that no two are parallel and no three pass through a common point. Prove that they divide the plane into $\dfrac{n^2+n+2}2$ regions.

## Hint

Add the lines one at a time and count how many new regions the last line creates.

## Solution

**Idea.** Let $R_n$ be the number of regions. Compare $R_{n+1}$ and $R_n$.

**Induction.** For $n=0$ there is one region, and $\frac{0+0+2}2=1$.

Suppose the formula holds for $n$ lines and add an $(n+1)$-th line $\ell$. It crosses each of the $n$ existing lines at $n$ distinct points. These $n$ points cut $\ell$ into $n+1$ pieces.

Each piece lies inside one existing region and splits it into two. Thus exactly $n+1$ new regions are created:
$$R_{n+1}=R_n+n+1.$$

**Computation.**
$$\frac{n^2+n+2}2+n+1=\frac{(n+1)^2+(n+1)+2}2,$$
which is the formula for $n+1$.

**Remark.** Equivalently $R_n=1+\binom n1+\binom n2$.

**Lesson.** To count regions or objects built incrementally, count the change produced by the last object added.
@@@ comb-mantel-s-theorem.md
---
title: Triangle-free graphs have at most n²/4 edges (Mantel)
category: combi/graphs
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: double counting, graphs, Cauchy–Schwarz, extremal graph theory
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

A graph on $n$ vertices has no triangle. Prove that it has at most $\dfrac{n^2}4$ edges. When is equality possible?

## Hint

If $u$ and $v$ are adjacent, can they have a common neighbour? Deduce a bound on $d(u)+d(v)$, then sum over all edges.

## Solution

**Idea.** Count the pairs (vertex, edge containing it) in two ways.

**Local bound.** Let $uv$ be an edge. If a vertex $w$ were adjacent to both $u$ and $v$, then $uvw$ would be a triangle. So the neighbourhoods of $u$ and $v$ are disjoint, and
$$d(u)+d(v)\le n.$$

**Summation.** Let $e$ be the number of edges. Summing over all edges $uv$,
$$\sum_{uv\in E}\bigl(d(u)+d(v)\bigr)\le ne.$$
On the left, each vertex $v$ appears $d(v)$ times, each with the term $d(v)$. So the left side equals $\sum_vd(v)^2$:
$$\sum_vd(v)^2\le ne.$$

**Cauchy–Schwarz.** Since $\sum_vd(v)=2e$,
$$\sum_vd(v)^2\ge\frac{\left(\sum_vd(v)\right)^2}n=\frac{4e^2}n.$$

**Conclusion.** $\dfrac{4e^2}n\le ne$, so $e\le\dfrac{n^2}4$.

**Equality.** For $n$ even, the complete bipartite graph $K_{n/2,n/2}$ is triangle-free with $\frac{n^2}4$ edges. For $n$ odd, the bound is $\left\lfloor\frac{n^2}4\right\rfloor$ and is attained by $K_{(n-1)/2,(n+1)/2}$.

**Lesson.** Double counting: when a sum over edges involves degrees, rewrite it as a sum over vertices; combine with Cauchy–Schwarz.
@@@ comb-leaves-of-a-tree.md
---
title: A tree has at least as many leaves as its maximum degree
category: combi/graphs
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: trees, graphs, handshake lemma, degrees
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

A tree is a connected graph without cycles. A leaf is a vertex of degree $1$.

1. Prove that a tree with $n\ge2$ vertices has exactly $n-1$ edges.
2. Prove that if a tree has a vertex of degree $k$, then it has at least $k$ leaves.

## Hint

For part 1, remove a leaf. For part 2, use the handshake lemma $\sum_v\deg v=2\times\#\text{edges}$.

## Solution

**1.** First, a tree with $n\ge2$ vertices has a leaf: take a longest path $v_0v_1\dots v_m$ in the tree. The vertex $v_0$ has no neighbour outside the path and no neighbour $v_i$ with $i\ge2$ (that would create a cycle), so its only neighbour is $v_1$.

Now induct on $n$. For $n=2$ the tree is one edge. For $n>2$, remove a leaf and its edge: the remaining graph is still connected and acyclic, with $n-1$ vertices and, by induction, $n-2$ edges. So the original tree has $n-1$ edges.

**2.** Let $v$ be a vertex of degree $k\ge1$. If $k=1$, then the tree has at least one leaf (indeed at least $2$ when $n\ge2$), so the claim $L\ge k$ holds trivially. Assume $k\ge2$. Let $L$ be the number of leaves. Each leaf has degree $1$; $v$ has degree $k$; all other $n-L-1$ vertices are not leaves and have degree at least $2$. By the handshake lemma and part 1,
$$2(n-1)=\sum_u\deg u\ge L+k+2(n-L-1).$$
Simplifying the right side: $2n-L-2+k$. Therefore $2n-2\ge2n-L-2+k$, i.e. $L\ge k$.

**Equality.** It holds when all vertices other than $v$ and the leaves have degree exactly $2$, that is when the tree is a "spider": paths glued at $v$.

**Lesson.** Handshake lemma: relate the degrees to the number of edges.
@@@ comb-every-tournament-has-a-hamiltonian-path.md
---
title: Every tournament has a Hamiltonian path
category: combi/graphs
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: induction, tournaments, graphs, Hamiltonian path
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

In a tournament, $n$ players play each other exactly once and there are no draws. Prove that the players can be ordered $P_1,P_2,\dots,P_n$ so that $P_1$ beat $P_2$, $P_2$ beat $P_3$, and so on up to $P_{n-1}$ beat $P_n$.

## Hint

Induction on $n$: remove one player, order the others, and find a place where the removed player can be inserted.

## Solution

**Idea.** Insert the last player into an ordering of the others.

**Base case.** For $n=1$ there is nothing to prove.

**Induction step.** Take a tournament with $n+1$ players and remove a player $v$. By the induction hypothesis the other $n$ players can be ordered $P_1,\dots,P_n$ with $P_i$ beating $P_{i+1}$ for all $i$. We insert $v$.
- If $v$ beat $P_1$, put $v$ first.
- If $P_n$ beat $v$, put $v$ last.
- Otherwise $P_1$ beat $v$ and $v$ beat $P_n$. Let $j$ be the smallest index with $v$ beating $P_j$. It exists since $v$ beats $P_n$, and $j\ge2$. By minimality of $j$, $P_{j-1}$ beat $v$. Insert $v$ between $P_{j-1}$ and $P_j$.

In all cases we obtain the required ordering of all $n+1$ players.

**Remark.** Rédei's theorem (1934).

**Lesson.** Induction on the number of vertices: delete one, apply the hypothesis, and find a flexible place to reinsert.
@@@ comb-coins-on-a-round-table.md
---
title: The game of coins on a round table
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: games, symmetry, winning strategy
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Two players take turns placing identical round coins on a circular table. Each coin must lie completely on the table and must not overlap the coins already placed (touching is allowed). The player who cannot place a coin loses. (A coin fits on the table.) Prove that the first player has a winning strategy.

## Hint

Use the centre of the table. The first player should have a reply to every possible move of the second one.

## Solution

**Idea.** A symmetry strategy: after the first move, always answer by the symmetric move.

**Strategy.** Let $O$ be the centre of the table and $r$ the radius of a coin. The first player puts his first coin with its centre at $O$. Afterwards, each time the opponent places a coin with centre $P$, the first player places a coin with centre $P'$, the reflection of $P$ in $O$.

**Invariant.** Just before each move of the second player, the position is symmetric with respect to $O$.

**The reply is legal.** Let the second player place a coin at $P$ (legal, so $P\ne O$).
- *On the table:* the table is symmetric about $O$, so the coin at $P'$ lies completely on the table.
- *No overlap with the coin at $P$:* two coins overlap iff their centres are at distance less than $2r$. Here $PP'=2\,OP\ge2r$ because the move at $P$ was legal and $O$ is occupied.
- *No overlap with the other coins:* by symmetry, if the coin at $P'$ overlapped some coin $C$, then the coin at $P$ would overlap the reflection of $C$: impossible.

**Conclusion.** The first player always has a legal reply. Since the table has finite area, the game ends after finitely many moves, and the player who cannot move is the second one.

**Lesson.** Symmetry strategy: if the board has a central symmetry, occupy the centre and mirror every move of the opponent.
@@@ comb-two-take-away-games.md
---
title: Two take-away games
category: combi/counting
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: games, winning positions, pairing strategy, Nim
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

1. A pile contains $n$ stones. Two players alternately remove between $1$ and $5$ stones. The player who takes the last stone wins. For which $n$ does the first player have a winning strategy?
2. There are two piles with the same number $m\ge1$ of stones. Two players alternately remove any positive number of stones from one pile of their choice. The player who takes the last stone wins. Who wins?

## Hint

For part 1, find the positions that are lost for the player to move, starting from small $n$. For part 2, think of a pairing strategy.

## Solution

**1. Winning positions.** Positions $n=1,\dots,5$ are winning for the player to move. The position $n=6$ is losing. We claim the first player loses if and only if $6\mid n$.

- *If $6\mid n$:* the second player answers each move "remove $k$" by "remove $6-k$", which is legal. After each of his moves the number of stones is a multiple of $6$. The first player can never take the last stone.
- *If $6\nmid n$:* the first player removes $r=n\bmod6\in\{1,\dots,5\}$ stones, leaving a multiple of $6$ and then plays the second player's strategy above.

**Answer 1.** The first player wins iff $n$ is not a multiple of $6$.

**2. Pairing.** The second player wins. After each move of the first player, he removes the same number of stones from the other pile. Then the piles are equal after each of his moves. The first player can never take the last stone; the second player finishes by taking the last stones of the second pile.

**Remark.** These are the simplest cases of impartial game theory (Sprague–Grundy); the general sum-of-piles result is Bouton's solution of Nim.

**Lesson.** Compute the losing positions from the end of the game, then find a complement strategy.
@@@ comb-subsets-without-two-consecutive-integers.md
---
title: Subsets without two consecutive integers
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: counting, recurrences, Fibonacci, bijection
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

1. Let $a_n$ be the number of subsets of $\{1,2,\dots,n\}$ (including the empty set) that contain no two consecutive integers. Prove that $a_n=F_{n+2}$, where $F_1=F_2=1$ and $F_{k+2}=F_{k+1}+F_k$.
2. Prove that the number of $k$-element subsets of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ without two consecutive integers is $\dbinom{n-k+1}k$.

## Hint

For part 1, split according to whether $n$ belongs to the subset. For part 2, subtract $i-1$ from the $i$-th smallest element.

## Solution

**1. Recurrence.** Take $n\ge2$ and split the admissible subsets of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ into two classes.
- Those not containing $n$: they are exactly the admissible subsets of $\{1,\dots,n-1\}$, so there are $a_{n-1}$.
- Those containing $n$: they do not contain $n-1$, and removing $n$ leaves an admissible subset of $\{1,\dots,n-2\}$; conversely adding $n$ to such a subset is admissible. So there are $a_{n-2}$.

Hence $a_n=a_{n-1}+a_{n-2}$. The initial values are $a_0=1$ and $a_1=2$, which are $F_2$ and $F_3$. By induction $a_n=F_{n+2}$.

**2. Bijection.** Let $x_1<x_2<\dots<x_k$ be an admissible subset, so $x_{i+1}\ge x_i+2$. Define $y_i=x_i-(i-1)$. Then $y_{i+1}-y_i\ge1$, so $y_1<\dots<y_k$, and $1\le y_1$, $y_k\le n-k+1$. Conversely, from any $k$-subset $y_1<\dots<y_k$ of $\{1,\dots,n-k+1\}$, setting $x_i=y_i+(i-1)$ gives an admissible subset. These maps are inverse, so the count is $\binom{n-k+1}k$.

**Check.** Summing over $k$ gives $\sum_k\binom{n-k+1}k=F_{n+2}$.

**Lesson.** Recurrence by conditioning on the last element, and bijections that "squeeze out" a constraint.
@@@ comb-derangements-by-inclusion-exclusion.md
---
title: Derangements and the number 1/e
category: combi/inclusion-exclusion
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: inclusion-exclusion, permutations, derangements
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

A derangement of $\{1,\dots,n\}$ is a permutation with no fixed point. Let $D_n$ be their number.

1. Prove that $D_n=n!\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}$.
2. Compute $D_4$, and show that for $n\ge2$, $D_n$ is the integer closest to $\dfrac{n!}e$.

## Hint

Let $A_i$ be the set of permutations fixing $i$. Apply inclusion–exclusion to $A_1\cup\dots\cup A_n$.

## Solution

**1. Inclusion–exclusion.** $D_n=n!-\left|A_1\cup\dots\cup A_n\right|$. For a set $I$ of $k$ indices, the permutations fixing every element of $I$ number $(n-k)!$. There are $\binom nk$ such sets. Therefore
$$D_n=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\binom nk(n-k)!=\sum_{k=0}^n(-1)^k\frac{n!}{k!}=n!\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}.$$

**2. Values.** $D_4=24-24+12-4+1=9$.

**Closest integer.** Since $e^{-1}=\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}$,
$$\frac{n!}e-D_n=n!\sum_{k>n}\frac{(-1)^k}{k!}.$$
The series is alternating with decreasing terms, so its absolute value is at most the first term: $\left|\frac{n!}e-D_n\right|\le\frac{n!}{(n+1)!}=\frac1{n+1}\le\frac13<\frac12$ for $n\ge2$. So $D_n$ is the integer closest to $\frac{n!}e$.

**Consequence.** The probability that a random permutation has no fixed point tends to $\frac1e$.

**Lesson.** Inclusion–exclusion: when the intersections of the bad events all have the same size depending only on the number of events, the sum collapses to a clean formula.
@@@ comb-the-sum-of-squared-binomial-coefficients.md
---
title: A combinatorial proof that Σ C(n,k)² = C(2n,n)
category: combi/binomial
level: m1
difficulty: 2
tags: double counting, binomial identities, Vandermonde
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Prove that for every $n\ge1$,
$$\sum_{k=0}^n\binom nk^2=\binom{2n}n.$$

## Hint

Count in two ways the committees of $n$ people chosen from a group of $n$ men and $n$ women.

## Solution

**Idea.** Interpret both sides as the number of ways of choosing $n$ people among $2n$.

**Right side.** Among $n$ men and $n$ women, the number of committees of $n$ people is $\binom{2n}n$.

**Left side.** Classify by the number $k$ of men, with $0\le k\le n$. Then there are $n-k$ women. The number of such committees is
$$\binom nk\binom n{n-k}=\binom nk^2,$$
using $\binom n{n-k}=\binom nk$. Summing over $k$ gives the left side.

**Check.** $n=3$: $1+9+9+1=20=\binom63$.

**Generalisation.** Vandermonde's identity $\sum_k\binom mk\binom n{r-k}=\binom{m+n}r$.

**Lesson.** Double counting: to prove an identity, find a set whose size can be computed in two different ways.
@@@ comb-paths-that-never-go-below-the-axis-catalan.md
---
title: Paths that never go below the axis: Catalan numbers
category: combi/binomial
level: olympiad
difficulty: 4
tags: reflection principle, Catalan numbers, lattice paths, bijection
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

A path of length $2n$ starts at height $0$ and at each step goes up by $1$ or down by $1$. Prove that the number of such paths that end at height $0$ and never go below height $0$ is
$$C_n=\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}n.$$

## Hint

Count all paths from $0$ to $0$, then subtract the bad ones. For a bad path, reflect the part before the first visit to height $-1$.

## Solution

**Idea.** The reflection principle: bad paths are in bijection with paths that are easy to count.

**All paths.** A path from $0$ to $0$ with $2n$ steps has $n$ up-steps and $n$ down-steps, so there are $\binom{2n}n$ of them.

**Bad paths.** A path from $0$ to $0$ is bad if it reaches height $-1$ at some moment. Consider the first such moment $t$, and reflect the portion of the path before $t$ about the line $y=-1$. The new path starts at $-2$, still reaches $-1$ first at time $t$, and ends at $0$.

Conversely, any path from $-2$ to $0$ with $2n$ steps must cross $-1$; reflecting the part before the first visit to $-1$ gives a bad path from $0$ to $0$. These operations are inverse, so bad paths are in bijection with all paths from $-2$ to $0$.

**Counting those.** A path from $-2$ to $0$ in $2n$ steps has $u$ up-steps and $d$ down-steps with $u+d=2n$ and $u-d=2$: $u=n+1$, $d=n-1$. So there are $\binom{2n}{n+1}$ of them.

**Conclusion.**
$$C_n=\binom{2n}n-\binom{2n}{n+1}=\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}n.$$
The first values are $1,2,5,14,42,132,\dots$

**Remark.** The same numbers count well-formed sequences of $n$ pairs of brackets and triangulations of a convex $(n+2)$-gon.

**Lesson.** Reflection principle: reflect an initial segment to transform a constrained count into an unconstrained one.
@@@ comb-flipping-rows-and-columns-of-a-board.md
---
title: Flipping rows and columns of a board
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: invariant, parity, colouring, moves
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

The squares of an $n\times n$ board ($n\ge2$) are all white. A move chooses one row or one column and changes the colour of every square in it. Prove that it is impossible to obtain a board with exactly one black square.

## Hint

Look at a $2\times2$ block formed by two rows and two columns, and count its black squares modulo $2$.

## Solution

**Idea.** Find a quantity that no move can change.

**Invariant.** Fix two rows $r\ne r'$ and two columns $c\ne c'$, and consider the four squares $(r,c),(r,c'),(r',c),(r',c')$. Look at the parity of the number of black squares among these four.
- A move on row $r$ (or $r'$) changes exactly two of them.
- A move on column $c$ (or $c'$) likewise changes exactly two.
- A move on any other row or column changes none of them.

Changing two squares changes their black count by $-2$, $0$ or $+2$: the parity of the black count is invariant.

**Conclusion.** At the start this number is $0$, so it stays even forever. Now suppose a board with a single black square $(a,b)$ could be reached. Choose any other row $r'\ne a$ and column $c'\ne b$ and the block of the rows $a,r'$ and columns $b,c'$: it contains exactly one black square, an odd number. Contradiction.

**Remark.** The reachable boards are exactly those in which every $2\times2$ block has an even number of black squares.

**Lesson.** Invariant: restrict attention to a small sub-configuration on which each move acts in a controlled way.
@@@ comb-finding-the-heavy-coin-with-a-balance.md
---
title: Finding the heavy coin with a balance
category: combi/counting
level: olympiad
difficulty: 3
tags: algorithms, information, induction, weighings
date: 2026-11-04
---

## Statement

Among $N$ coins, exactly one is heavier than the others, which all have the same weight. You may use a balance scale (comparing the weights of two groups, with three possible outcomes: left heavier, right heavier, balance).

1. Prove that if $N=3^k$, the heavy coin can always be found in $k$ weighings.
2. Prove that if $N>3^k$, it is not always possible to find it in $k$ weighings.

## Hint

For part 1, divide the coins into three equal groups. For part 2, count the possible sequences of outcomes.

## Solution

**1. Strategy.** Induction on $k$. For $k=0$ there is a single coin. For $k\ge1$, divide the $3^k$ coins into three groups $A,B,C$ of $3^{k-1}$ coins and weigh $A$ against $B$.
- If $A$ is heavier, the heavy coin is in $A$; if $B$ is heavier, it is in $B$.
- If the scale balances, it is in $C$.

In each case, the heavy coin is among $3^{k-1}$ coins, which can be found in $k-1$ further weighings by the induction hypothesis. In total $k$ weighings suffice.

**2. Lower bound.** Fix a strategy using at most $k$ weighings. Each possible heavy coin gives a sequence of at most $k$ outcomes, each outcome being one of $3$ values. There are at most $3^k$ such sequences.

If two different coins gave the same sequence, the strategy could not tell them apart. So a strategy that always finds the coin must give distinct sequences to distinct coins, hence $N\le3^k$. Therefore $N>3^k$ coins cannot be handled in $k$ weighings.

**Remark.** An information-theoretic argument: each weighing provides at most $\log_23$ bits of information. The harder variant with a coin that may be heavier or lighter is solved by the same principle: $12$ coins in $3$ weighings.

**Lesson.** Optimal algorithm problems come in two halves: an explicit strategy, and a counting argument on outcomes showing that nothing better is possible.
